# Unit 10 linguistic and owned-edition mapping packet

This packet prepares review. It records an unsigned structural mapping proposal. It does not record a qualified review, an approved edition alignment, or an install decision.

The owned-edition mapping worksheet separates observed edition facts, the proposed lesson and printed page range, unresolved edition questions, and matters that still require qualified linguistic review. ISBN, owned-copy verification, variance, reviewer identity, qualification, date, and approval stay blank. The disposition remains PENDING_CURRICULUM_REVIEWER.

Unit 10 remains in development. Its readiness is content-complete and review-pending. The Lesson 10 relationship is a proposed, unsigned mapping. It is not an approved edition alignment.

Recording a review status, reviewer signature, mapping approval, install state, or readiness does not change it.

`mappingPackageDigest` binds the proposed edition-mapping facts for Unit 10: edition identity, lesson, printed page range, topic description, observation source, ISBN observation state, and the pending disposition. Reviewer identity, qualification, date, notes, correction notes, and signature stay outside that digest.

`packContentReviewDigest` is the repository aggregate of instructional curriculum. It is not an approval of this unit, and it is not part of the review-package digest.

`reviewPackageDigest` identifies this complete pending review package. It binds the ordered instructional records and the mapping-package digest. Changing a proposed page range or topic changes the mapping-package digest and therefore this package digest.

`repositorySourceDigest` binds repository and release inputs. Recording an approval later changes that repository hash. That change does not replace the signed content-review digest or the mapping-package digest.

The marker scan used by this package detects only a fixed list of known prohibited mapping-input markers. It is not comprehensive copyright detection and cannot recognize every copy or close paraphrase of protected material.

- Objectives: obj-u10-absence

- Source: src-wikibooks-russian-cc-by-sa

- Content hash: 3fcf5d01ada20f658fe4a64a0cc7de4df1065bdf3945cfdb5e82343379eb69a2

recordType=meaning
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Grammar/Genitive_case?oldid=4567338
sourceRevision=4567338
sourceRecordId=genitive:Another use of the genitive is in the phrase "I have...". Though Russian does ha
originalSourceText=Another use of the genitive is in the phrase "I have...". Though Russian does have the verb 'to have' ('''иметь'''), it is rarely used. Instead, Russians use the following construction to say "I have X": '''У меня есть Х'''.
This literally means, 'By me exists X'. Since the preposition '''у''' calls the genitive case, the person or thing that has the object in question is placed in the genitive. The thing that is possessed remains in the nominative: '''У''' + ''gen'' + '''есть''' + ''nom''
To turn this into a question, place an inflection on '''есть''', just as you place an inflection on the verb in a normal question.
:Do you have a son? - '''У вас есть сын?'''
:Yes, I have a son - '''Да, у меня есть сын.'''
:Yes, I do - '''Да, есть.'''

'''Есть''' on its own can also be used to say 'There is...', 'There are...', or 'There exists...'. It is followed by the nominative case:
:There are Frenchmen on this street - '''На э́той у́лице есть францу́зы'''
:There are old buildings in Moscow - '''В Москве́ есть ста́рые зда́ния'''

Conversely, to say 'There isn't...', 'There aren't...', we use the word '''нет''' followed by the genitive case. Note that this use of the word '''нет''' is quite different from its usual meaning of 'no', and is closer to its original meaning as a contraction of '''не есть'''.
:There are no restaurants - '''Нет рестора́нов'''
:There are no old buildings in Moscow - '''В Москве́ нет ста́рых зда́ний'''

So, combining the above, to say 'I ''don't'' have...' is constructed as ''''У меня нет...'''', followed by the ''genitive'' case.
:Do you have a son? - '''У вас есть сын?'''
:No, I don't have a son - '''У меня́ нет сы́на'''
:My brother doesn't have any money - '''У моего́ бра́та нет де́нег
shippedText=No, I don't have a son
transformation=selection
licenseId=CC BY-SA 4.0
licenseUrl=https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/

recordType=example
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Grammar/Genitive_case?oldid=4567338
sourceRevision=4567338
sourceRecordId=genitive:Another use of the genitive is in the phrase "I have...". Though Russian does ha
originalSourceText=Another use of the genitive is in the phrase "I have...". Though Russian does have the verb 'to have' ('''иметь'''), it is rarely used. Instead, Russians use the following construction to say "I have X": '''У меня есть Х'''.
This literally means, 'By me exists X'. Since the preposition '''у''' calls the genitive case, the person or thing that has the object in question is placed in the genitive. The thing that is possessed remains in the nominative: '''У''' + ''gen'' + '''есть''' + ''nom''
To turn this into a question, place an inflection on '''есть''', just as you place an inflection on the verb in a normal question.
:Do you have a son? - '''У вас есть сын?'''
:Yes, I have a son - '''Да, у меня есть сын.'''
:Yes, I do - '''Да, есть.'''

'''Есть''' on its own can also be used to say 'There is...', 'There are...', or 'There exists...'. It is followed by the nominative case:
:There are Frenchmen on this street - '''На э́той у́лице есть францу́зы'''
:There are old buildings in Moscow - '''В Москве́ есть ста́рые зда́ния'''

Conversely, to say 'There isn't...', 'There aren't...', we use the word '''нет''' followed by the genitive case. Note that this use of the word '''нет''' is quite different from its usual meaning of 'no', and is closer to its original meaning as a contraction of '''не есть'''.
:There are no restaurants - '''Нет рестора́нов'''
:There are no old buildings in Moscow - '''В Москве́ нет ста́рых зда́ний'''

So, combining the above, to say 'I ''don't'' have...' is constructed as ''''У меня нет...'''', followed by the ''genitive'' case.
:Do you have a son? - '''У вас есть сын?'''
:No, I don't have a son - '''У меня́ нет сы́на'''
:My brother doesn't have any money - '''У моего́ бра́та нет де́нег
shippedText=У меня́ нет сы́на
transformation=selection

licenseUrl=https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/

originalSourceText=Another use of the genitive is in the phrase "I have...". Though Russian does have the verb 'to have' ('''иметь'''), it is rarely used. Instead, Russians use the following construction to say "I have X": '''У меня есть Х'''.
This literally means, 'By me exists X'. Since the preposition '''у''' calls the genitive case, the person or thing that has the object in question is placed in the genitive. The thing that is possessed remains in the nominative: '''У''' + ''gen'' + '''есть''' + ''nom''
To turn this into a question, place an inflection on '''есть''', just as you place an inflection on the verb in a normal question.
:Do you have a son? - '''У вас есть сын?'''
:Yes, I have a son - '''Да, у меня есть сын.'''
:Yes, I do - '''Да, есть.'''

'''Есть''' on its own can also be used to say 'There is...', 'There are...', or 'There exists...'. It is followed by the nominative case:
:There are Frenchmen on this street - '''На э́той у́лице есть францу́зы'''
:There are old buildings in Moscow - '''В Москве́ есть ста́рые зда́ния'''

Conversely, to say 'There isn't...', 'There aren't...', we use the word '''нет''' followed by the genitive case. Note that this use of the word '''нет''' is quite different from its usual meaning of 'no', and is closer to its original meaning as a contraction of '''не есть'''.
:There are no restaurants - '''Нет рестора́нов'''
:There are no old buildings in Moscow - '''В Москве́ нет ста́рых зда́ний'''

So, combining the above, to say 'I ''don't'' have...' is constructed as ''''У меня нет...'''', followed by the ''genitive'' case.
:Do you have a son? - '''У вас есть сын?'''
:No, I don't have a son - '''У меня́ нет сы́на'''
:My brother doesn't have any money - '''У моего́ бра́та нет де́нег
shippedText=Yes, I have a son
transformation=selection

recordType=meaning
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Grammar/Genitive_case?oldid=4567338
sourceRevision=4567338
sourceRecordId=genitive:Another use of the genitive is in the phrase "I have...". Though Russian does ha
originalSourceText=Another use of the genitive is in the phrase "I have...". Though Russian does have the verb 'to have' ('''иметь'''), it is rarely used. Instead, Russians use the following construction to say "I have X": '''У меня есть Х'''.
This literally means, 'By me exists X'. Since the preposition '''у''' calls the genitive case, the person or thing that has the object in question is placed in the genitive. The thing that is possessed remains in the nominative: '''У''' + ''gen'' + '''есть''' + ''nom''
To turn this into a question, place an inflection on '''есть''', just as you place an inflection on the verb in a normal question.
:Do you have a son? - '''У вас есть сын?'''
:Yes, I have a son - '''Да, у меня есть сын.'''
:Yes, I do - '''Да, есть.'''

'''Есть''' on its own can also be used to say 'There is...', 'There are...', or 'There exists...'. It is followed by the nominative case:
:There are Frenchmen on this street - '''На э́той у́лице есть францу́зы'''
:There are old buildings in Moscow - '''В Москве́ есть ста́рые зда́ния'''

Conversely, to say 'There isn't...', 'There aren't...', we use the word '''нет''' followed by the genitive case. Note that this use of the word '''нет''' is quite different from its usual meaning of 'no', and is closer to its original meaning as a contraction of '''не есть'''.
:There are no restaurants - '''Нет рестора́нов'''
:There are no old buildings in Moscow - '''В Москве́ нет ста́рых зда́ний'''

So, combining the above, to say 'I ''don't'' have...' is constructed as ''''У меня нет...'''', followed by the ''genitive'' case.
:Do you have a son? - '''У вас есть сын?'''
:No, I don't have a son - '''У меня́ нет сы́на'''
:My brother doesn't have any money - '''У моего́ бра́та нет де́нег
shippedText=I have X
transformation=selection
licenseId=CC BY-SA 4.0
licenseUrl=https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/
attribution=Wikibooks Russian grammar contributors, CC BY-SA 4.0. Page history for each frozen oldid identifies the contributors.
shareAlikeTreatment=Adapted curriculum data, selection, and arrangement are distributed under CC BY-SA 4.0. Application code, user data, and user recordings are outside that statement. No audio is relicensed because this unit ships no audio.

recordType=example
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Grammar/Genitive_case?oldid=4567338
sourceRevision=4567338
sourceRecordId=genitive:Another use of the genitive is in the phrase "I have...". Though Russian does ha
originalSourceText=Another use of the genitive is in the phrase "I have...". Though Russian does have the verb 'to have' ('''иметь'''), it is rarely used. Instead, Russians use the following construction to say "I have X": '''У меня есть Х'''.
This literally means, 'By me exists X'. Since the preposition '''у''' calls the genitive case, the person or thing that has the object in question is placed in the genitive. The thing that is possessed remains in the nominative: '''У''' + ''gen'' + '''есть''' + ''nom''
To turn this into a question, place an inflection on '''есть''', just as you place an inflection on the verb in a normal question.
:Do you have a son? - '''У вас есть сын?'''
:Yes, I have a son - '''Да, у меня есть сын.'''
:Yes, I do - '''Да, есть.'''

'''Есть''' on its own can also be used to say 'There is...', 'There are...', or 'There exists...'. It is followed by the nominative case:
:There are Frenchmen on this street - '''На э́той у́лице есть францу́зы'''
:There are old buildings in Moscow - '''В Москве́ есть ста́рые зда́ния'''

Conversely, to say 'There isn't...', 'There aren't...', we use the word '''нет''' followed by the genitive case. Note that this use of the word '''нет''' is quite different from its usual meaning of 'no', and is closer to its original meaning as a contraction of '''не есть'''.
:There are no restaurants - '''Нет рестора́нов'''
:There are no old buildings in Moscow - '''В Москве́ нет ста́рых зда́ний'''

So, combining the above, to say 'I ''don't'' have...' is constructed as ''''У меня нет...'''', followed by the ''genitive'' case.
:Do you have a son? - '''У вас есть сын?'''
:No, I don't have a son - '''У меня́ нет сы́на'''
:My brother doesn't have any money - '''У моего́ бра́та нет де́нег
shippedText=Да
transformation=selection

:There are Frenchmen on this street - '''На э́той у́лице есть францу́зы'''
:There are old buildings in Moscow - '''В Москве́ есть ста́рые зда́ния'''

Conversely, to say 'There isn't...', 'There aren't...', we use the word '''нет''' followed by the genitive case. Note that this use of the word '''нет''' is quite different from its usual meaning of 'no', and is closer to its original meaning as a contraction of '''не есть'''.
:There are no restaurants - '''Нет рестора́нов'''
:There are no old buildings in Moscow - '''В Москве́ нет ста́рых зда́ний'''

So, combining the above, to say 'I ''don't'' have...' is constructed as ''''У меня нет...'''', followed by the ''genitive'' case.
:Do you have a son? - '''У вас есть сын?'''
:No, I don't have a son - '''У меня́ нет сы́на'''
:My brother doesn't have any money - '''У моего́ бра́та нет де́нег
shippedText=У моего́ бра́та нет де́нег

- Content hash: fac0b2a05c0a7ad4972330c356d22f205b3cc14f3c04846c6cc854f5282bc048

recordType=instruction
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Grammar/Genitive_case?oldid=4567338
sourceRevision=4567338
sourceRecordId=genitive:Another use of the genitive is in the phrase "I have...". Though Russian does ha
originalSourceText=Another use of the genitive is in the phrase "I have...". Though Russian does have the verb 'to have' ('''иметь'''), it is rarely used. Instead, Russians use the following construction to say "I have X": '''У меня есть Х'''.
This literally means, 'By me exists X'. Since the preposition '''у''' calls the genitive case, the person or thing that has the object in question is placed in the genitive. The thing that is possessed remains in the nominative: '''У''' + ''gen'' + '''есть''' + ''nom''
To turn this into a question, place an inflection on '''есть''', just as you place an inflection on the verb in a normal question.
:Do you have a son? - '''У вас есть сын?'''
:Yes, I have a son - '''Да, у меня есть сын.'''
:Yes, I do - '''Да, есть.'''

'''Есть''' on its own can also be used to say 'There is...', 'There are...', or 'There exists...'. It is followed by the nominative case:
:There are Frenchmen on this street - '''На э́той у́лице есть францу́зы'''
:There are old buildings in Moscow - '''В Москве́ есть ста́рые зда́ния'''

Conversely, to say 'There isn't...', 'There aren't...', we use the word '''нет''' followed by the genitive case. Note that this use of the word '''нет''' is quite different from its usual meaning of 'no', and is closer to its original meaning as a contraction of '''не есть'''.
:There are no restaurants - '''Нет рестора́нов'''
:There are no old buildings in Moscow - '''В Москве́ нет ста́рых зда́ний'''

So, combining the above, to say 'I ''don't'' have...' is constructed as ''''У меня нет...'''', followed by the ''genitive'' case.
:Do you have a son? - '''У вас есть сын?'''
:No, I don't have a son - '''У меня́ нет сы́на'''
:My brother doesn't have any money - '''У моего́ бра́та нет де́нег

:Do you have a son? - '''У вас есть сын?'''
:Yes, I have a son - '''Да, у меня есть сын.'''
:Yes, I do - '''Да, есть.'''

:There are Frenchmen on this street - '''На э́той у́лице есть францу́зы'''
:There are old buildings in Moscow - '''В Москве́ есть ста́рые зда́ния'''

:There are no restaurants - '''Нет рестора́нов'''
:There are no old buildings in Moscow - '''В Москве́ нет ста́рых зда́ний'''

So, combining the above, to say 'I ''don't'' have...' is constructed as ''''У меня нет...'''', followed by the ''genitive'' case.
:Do you have a son? - '''У вас есть сын?'''
:No, I don't have a son - '''У меня́ нет сы́на'''
:My brother doesn't have any money - '''У моего́ бра́та нет де́нег
